Let me think about the proof strategy. This is the classic locally nameless STLC strong normalization proof. The target is CBV strong normalization.

The standard approach: logical relation indexed by type, where:
- `R_Bool(t) := SN(t)` (strongly normalizing)
- `R_{T1 -> T2}(t) := SN(t) /\ forall u, R_{T1}(u) -> R_{T2}(app t u)` — but for CBV we need the closure under reduction.

Actually for strong normalization of CBV STLC, the classic logical relation: define `SN t` as the inductive strongly_normalizing, then define `LR T t`:
- `LR Bool t := SN t`
- `LR (T1 -> T2) t := SN t /\ forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app t u)`.

Hmm, but to prove the fundamental theorem for abstraction: given `forall x, LR T2 (open t (fvar x))` under context, show `LR (T1->T2) (abs T1 t)`. Need:
1. SN (abs T1 t) — since its only steps come from ST_AppAbs with a value argument, and we need SN of open t v. The body's SN under substitution of arbitrary value.
2. For all u with LR T1 u, LR T2 (app (abs T1 t) u). We need to show app (abs T1 t) u is SN and its application closure.

The key issue with the simple LR: strong normalization of `app (abs T1 t) u` requires SN of `open t u` and closure properties. This is the standard "beta-expansion" or "CR" argument. There is a standard approach: use the inductive definition of SN and prove properties like "if t is SN and t' is SN then app t t' is SN" etc. But ST_AppAbs requires locally_closed (abs T1 t) and value v. Hmm, in CBV the argument must be a value, so `app (abs T1 t) u` steps to `open t u` only when u is a value. So we need values in LR.

Alternatively, use Girard's reducibility candidates adapted: neutral terms. A standard approach for CBV strong normalization uses the "SN" inductive directly with a logical relation that includes:
- R_Bool t := SN t
- R_{A->B} t := SN t /\ forall u, R_A u -> R_B (app t u).

And we need "backward closure": if t steps to t' and R t' then R t? Hmm, not needed necessarily.

Let me think about the actual proof obligations.

Fundamental theorem: for Gamma well-formed? We need to consider terms with free variables and substitutions. Standard locally nameless: define the LR on open terms, prove:

1. `LR T t -> SN t` (included by definition for arrow; for Bool it's SN by def).
2. Value lemma: every value of type T is in LR T? We need "values of type T are in LR T"? Actually to handle beta, we need: if `forall x, LR T2 (open t (fvar x))` and `value v` and `LR T1 v`, then `LR T2 (open t v)`. This is substitution lemma for LR. Typically proved via the fundamental theorem for the open term with a context containing x: T1, using a "substitution of free variables" lemma.

The classic locally nameless SN proof (e.g., from Software Foundations? or the "locally nameless" tutorial by Charguéraud) does this. Let me recall the structure.

Actually there's a well-known approach: define `tm_sn` as an inductive with constructors for each term former (like `SN` as a "hereditarily terminating" predicate), then logical relation via `ty_relation` recursively:

```
Fixpoint TR (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => SN t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t => SN t /\ (forall u, TR T1 u -> TR T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```

Hmm, but SN for values and applications in CBV: SN (abs T1 t) means all its reducts are SN. Reducts of `abs` : none. So SN (abs ...) holds trivially! Indeed SN_intro with no successor: for all t', abs --> t' -> SN t'. For the abstraction case, we need `SN (tm_abs T1 t)` which is trivial, plus closure under application: for all u with TR T1 u, TR T2 (app (abs T1 t) u).

Now TR T2 (app (abs T1 t) u) means SN (app ...) plus more if T2 is arrow. SN (app (abs T1 t) u): possible steps: ST_AppAbs -> open t u (if value u), ST_App1 -> app t' u (but abs doesn't step), ST_App2 -> app (abs..) u' for u --> u'. So need SN (open t u) and for all u' with u --> u', SN (app (abs) u').

So we need: if `forall x, TR T2 (open t (fvar x))` and `TR T1 u` (which gives SN u), and `value u`?? Hmm, need to know open t u is in TR T2. The substitution lemma is:

`subst_TR : (forall x, TR T (open t (fvar x))) -> forall u, TR T u -> TR T (open t u)`? 

Wait that's not right either: u is a closed term being substituted, but open t u means substitute u for fvar 0? Actually `open_rec` replaces `tm_bvar k` with u, not free variables. Hmm, in this locally nameless formalization, `open t u` replaces bound variable 0 in t with u. And in T_Abs, the body is `open t1 (tm_fvar x)` — wait, that would replace bvar 0 with fvar x. That's unusual: typically `open_rec 0 (fvar x) t1` opens the outermost binder. And the inductively-defined `t1` in the abs is the "closed at binder"? Hmm.

Wait let's look carefully: `T_Abs : forall L Gamma T1 T2 t1, (forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (x |-> T1; Gamma) (open t1 (tm_fvar x)) T2) -> has_type Gamma (tm_abs T1 t1) (T1 -> T2)`. So `t1` is a term with a bvar 0 free (representing the bound variable), and `open t1 (tm_fvar x)` substitutes the free var x. That's standard locally nameless. `open_rec k u t` replaces bvar k with u. `open t u = open_rec 0 u t`.

And in ST_AppAbs: `app (abs T1 t) v --> open t v`. Wait, `open t v` = open_rec 0 v t, substituting v for bvar 0. Standard.

Also `lc_at` tracks that all bvars are < k. Note: for `abs T t`, `locally_closed (abs T t)` means `lc_at 1 t`. Good.

But note: `tm_app (abs T t) v --> open t v` requires `locally_closed (abs T t)` and `value v`. And ST_App1 requires `locally_closed t2`. Interesting: the semantics doesn't require v closed for ST_AppAbs (open t v could contain free vars from v; that's fine).

Let me design my own proof carefully.

Definitions:

```
Definition sn := strongly_normalizing.

Definition tr (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => sn
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t => sn t /\ forall u, tr T1 u -> tr T2 (tm_app t u)
  end.
```

Wait, `tr` recursive on T with a function returning Prop; fine with Fixpoint? The recursive call `tr T1 u` appears under a lambda, and `tr T1` must be structurally decreasing on T. Since T1 is a subterm of Ty_Arrow T1 T2, yes. But Coq requires `Fixpoint tr (T : ty) : tm -> Prop := match T with ... | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t => sn t /\ forall u, tr T1 u -> tr T2 (tm_app t u) end.` The recursive calls `tr T1` and `tr T2` are on subterms — but they're partially applied. Coq accepts higher-order recursion on subterms? The guard checker checks that recursive calls occur on structurally smaller arguments; here `tr T1 u` where `tr T1` is a partial application — I believe Coq's guard checker accepts `tr T1` as a recursive call because the principal argument T1 is a subterm. Actually when writing `tr T1 u`, the guard condition sees the recursive call with argument T1 which is a subterm of the matched T. Should be fine. Many developments define such LRs.

But careful: the arrow case must handle the CBV-specific behavior including that the argument might step before being a value. Let's reconsider.

Define `tr T t` such that:
- Bool: SN t.
- Arrow A B: SN t /\ forall u, tr A u -> tr B (app t u).

We want: if t is in tr (A->B) and u in tr A, then app t u in tr B. That's immediate from the definition (second component).

Fundamental theorem: for all Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> for all sigma (mapping atoms to terms) such that for all x T, Gamma x = Some T -> tr T (sigma x), and ... locally closed stuff? we have tr T (sigma x) -> SN (sigma x), we need substitution t[sigma] to be well-defined, and we need to prove tr T (subst sigma t).

For locally nameless, the fundamental theorem is about open terms with free variables: we prove `has_type Gamma t T -> forall sigma, (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> tr T (sigma x)) -> tr T (subst sigma t)` where subst replaces free vars. But abstractions need an extension to the body's bvar: the body t1 under `open t1 (fvar x)`. We need a substitution/simplification lemma about `open`.

Hmm. Alternatively, we can avoid defining free-variable substitution by working with the T_Abs rule directly: it uses opening with fvar x. Standard approach in LN: the substitution lemma is proved by induction on t and the induction uses the fact that free variables are "arbitrary" and can be instantiated. Something like: if for all x, tr T (open t (fvar x)), then for all u, ... — the trick is that free var substitution and bvar substitution commute/are the same when t has only bvar 0 free? No.

Hmm, actually the standard LN methodology: prove "open" lemmas: for a term `t` that has at most one free bvar (index 0) — the abs body — `open t u` substitutes u for bvar 0. The T_Abs rule gives typing for `open t1 (fvar x)` for fresh x. To get typing/substitution for arbitrary u, use the standard "substitution preserves typing" lemma:

`has_type (update Gamma x T1) t T2 -> has_type Gamma u T1 -> has_type Gamma (subst x u t) T2`? But this uses free-variable substitution subst, and then a relation between `subst x u (open t1 (fvar x))` and `open t1 u`. That's the standard "subst_open" lemma.

Hmm, but the T_Abs gives typing of `open t1 (fvar x)` under context update. Actually the fundamental theorem can be stated directly on the T_Abs hypothesis: we need to show tr (T1->T2) (abs T1 t1), which requires `forall u, tr T1 u -> tr T2 (app (abs T1 t1) u)`. Now, `tr T2 (app ... u)` requires SN (app ... u) and possibly more.

To prove SN (app (abs T1 t1) u) given SN u: this needs a lemma "if t1's opening with all fresh vars is SN then opening with u is SN" — the SN substitution lemma for bvars.

Actually, one standard way: prove `tr T (open t1 (fvar x))` for all x, then prove `tr T (open t1 u)` for all u in tr T, via the *fundamental theorem applied with a substitution mapping x to u*? Not exactly, because open t1 (fvar x) isn't of the form subst.

Let me reconsider. There are two styles:

Style A: Define the logical relation on locally-closed terms with free variables as atoms, and prove a "substitution" theorem for free variables: if Gamma |- t : T and sigma maps Gamma to tr, then tr T (t[sigma]). Then for the abs case: need tr (T1->T2) (abs T1 t1). Part 1 SN: trivial-ish. Part 2: for u with tr T1 u, show tr T2 (app (abs T1 t1) u). We know that `open t1 (fvar x)` is typed under x:T1 in all fresh x. Applying fundamental theorem with substitution sigma = [x -> u] gives tr T2 ((open t1 (fvar x))[x := u]) — and we need this to equal tr T2 (open t1 u). Indeed `(open t1 (fvar x))[x:=u] = open t1 u` when x not free in t1's variables? Hmm, open t1 (fvar x) could contain other free variables from t1. But t1 is a term where bvar 0 stands for the bound variable; other free vars come from... In the T_Abs rule, t1 is arbitrary syntax; there is no constraint that t1 has no other free vars. Since Gamma is a mapping of free vars, and the rule quantifies over x not in L, the free vars of t1 are in the context? Actually no constraint. But the substitution lemma needs to handle those other free vars too (they're arbitrary).

This is getting complicated because `open t1 (fvar x)` replaces bvar 0 with fvar x, and if we then substitute free vars including x by u, we get open t1 u but also need to substitute other free vars. Since the fundamental theorem is for all substitutions, that works out if we set up the statement right.

Style B (the one used in the LN tutorial for STLC SN, I believe): Define the logical relation and prove:
1. `tr T t -> sn t` for all T (by induction on T; arrow case needs SN included/exposed). We should define it so that tr T t -> sn t is easy: include sn as first component in arrow case.
2. `tr T t` is closed under expansion for values? Hmm.
3. The "body" lemma: `(forall x, tr T (open t (fvar x))) -> tr T (open t v)`? Not true for arbitrary v; needs v to be locally closed and tr? Let's test with T = Bool, t = bvar 0: open t (fvar x) = fvar x, which we'd need tr Bool (fvar x) = SN (fvar x) — true since fvar is a normal form (no steps). Then tr Bool v = SN v — not true for arbitrary v. So the lemma must be: `(forall x, tr T (open t (fvar x))) -> forall v, value v -> ... `? Still not: for t = bvar 0 and v = app (abs ... ) something non-SN? Can a non-SN closed value exist? Values are abs/true/false. Any abs is SN as a term (no rules step from abs), true/false SN. So all values are SN. But tr T v for arrow T requires more than SN.

Hmm wait, but we're substituting v into open, and open t v = v for t = bvar 0. So we need tr T v. The hypothesis `forall x, tr T (open t (fvar x))` = `forall x, tr T (fvar x)`. This says every free variable is in tr T. Can we conclude tr T v for arbitrary v? No, of course not. So we cannot prove the naive substitution lemma.

The fix: the logical relation should be defined so that "neutral" terms behave well and free variables are in every candidate. But substitution of arbitrary terms for a variable that ranges over all atoms: the hypothesis is a *forall over atoms*, and we need to instantiate with v. That's only possible if the candidate is "closed under substitution of related terms". Hmm.

The right framework: We should prove the fundamental theorem for terms *with free variables* where the context maps free variables to types, and the logical relation is on terms with free variables; the substitution of a free variable y with a term u is not needed if we directly prove "term in context has property". Inductive sn : tm -> Prop := ...

Let me consider a different, maybe much simpler approach: **Prove strong normalization via a translation to a well-founded measure / or via the "hereditary substitution" with an inductive SN predicate defined structurally, then show it implies the given strongly_normalizing.**

Actually, here's a classical clean approach for STLC CBV strong normalization: Use the *inductive* definition of SN and prove:

1. `sn_value : forall v, value v -> sn v` (all values are SN). For abs: SN_intro: abs --> t' is impossible (no rule has abs on the left with the abs as the whole term; ST_AppAbs applies to app; ST_App1/2 to app). So trivial by inversion.

2. `sn_app_inv` etc.

3. The crucial "beta expansion": if `sn t` (the argument) and `sn (open body t)`? Hmm.

Maybe better: Define the logical relation in terms of "sn of all applications" — the standard Tait-style for CBV:

Candidates for arrow type: sets C such that:
- C ⊆ SN
- if t ∈ C and u ∈ C_A (arg candidate), then app t u ∈ C_B? 

And neutral terms: In call-by-name, neutral SN terms are in candidates. For call-by-value, values of function type... In CBV, the argument to a function is a value at runtime; the "candidate" should satisfy: if t ∈ C_{A→B} then for all u ∈ C_A, app t u ∈ C_B. And fvar x ∈ C for all C? In CBV, is `fvar x` neutral and does it satisfy app fvar u ∈ C_B for u ∈ C_A? app fvar u is stuck SN. But then it needs to satisfy C_B = arrow candidate: for all w ∈ C_{A'}, app (app fvar u) w ∈ C_B'. Stuck applications are all SN and stuck. So we need a lemma: any term built from a variable by applications of related terms is in TR. Hmm, this can be handled if TR arrow candidate includes all terms t such that SN t and forall u, TR A u -> TR B (app t u), where we prove "stuck application" lemma by the fact that app (fvar x) u is such that its reducts are... wait, no reducts at all! SN is trivial, but TR B requires closure under all applications. And app(app(fvar x) u) w also has no steps... Since the term is stuck, TR T t for arbitrary T? For TR Bool t = SN t: yes since stuck. For TR (A->B) t: SN t (yes since t stuck) and forall u, TR A u -> TR B (app t u). app t u is still stuck. So by induction on? The structure of t grows, so we can't induct on t for the forall u part... but we can prove a lemma by induction on t: for a stuck term t with all its "components" SN-related... Hmm.

Actually here's the thing: define `tr T t` by recursion on T. Then prove `tr T t` for stuck terms by a *mutual induction on the type and the term*: We want `stuck_tr : forall T t, sn t -> (forall t', t --> t' -> tr T t') -> ...` no.

Let's think about the standard "reducibility candidate" definition for CBV. A candidate C of type A is a set of terms satisfying:
(C1) C ⊆ SN.
(C2) If t ∈ C and t --> t' then t' ∈ C? (backward/forward closure)
(C3) If t is neutral (stuck) and all its immediate reducts are in C, then t ∈ C? This handles variables.

But with `tr` defined by recursion on types and `sn` inductively, we can prove closure properties by induction on SN derivations.

Alternatively, avoid free variables entirely! Since the target is a closed-term theorem with empty context, maybe we can prove the fundamental theorem for *closed* terms only, and handle T_Abs via the cofinite quantification over all fresh x. The issue: the body t1 may have free variables not bound by x. But wait: if Gamma = empty and t1 has a free variable y, then open t1 (fvar x) has free y, and has_type (x|->T1; empty) (open t1 fvar x) T2 requires y in the context. The context is x|->T1; empty, which maps x to T1 and everything else None. If y ≠ x, then y not in context, so `T_Var y` requires None = Some T, impossible. If y = x, then x is bound by the abstraction, not free. So for Gamma = empty, the body cannot have free variables other than the bound one! Because the context only contains x. Hmm, but can the typing derivation "hide" a free variable? ### Core definitions

`strongly_normalizing` (SN) is given as an inductive:

```
Inductive strongly_normalizing : tm -> Prop :=
  | SN_intro : forall t, (forall t', t --> t' -> strongly_normalizing t') -> strongly_normalizing t.
```

Note this is the "all reducts are SN" definition, equivalent to well-foundedness of step from t. Properties:

- `sn_step : forall t t', SN t -> t --> t' -> SN t'` by inversion on SN t.
- `sn_norm : SN t -> ~ (exists t', t --> t')`? No, SN allows successors, just finite.

For constructing SN: `SN_intro`.

**Key lemma 1 (SN of stuck terms)**: If t has no successors (`forall t', ~ (t --> t')`), then SN t. `SN_intro` with the absurd.

**Lemma 2 (application SN composition)**: If SN t1 and SN t2 and (forall...)? We need to show SN (app t1 t2) in various cases. The step relation on app t1 t2 depends on t1's shape:
- If t1 = abs T b and t2 is a value: app steps to open b t2.
- If t1 --> t1': app --> app t1' t2 (requires locally_closed t2).
- If t1 is a value and t2 --> t2': app --> app t1 t2'.

So SN (app t1 t2) can be shown by a "strong induction on SN t1 and SN t2" but the ST_AppAbs case requires SN (open b t2). Since t1 = abs has no own reductions, SN t1 is trivially given, so the beta case is not covered by induction on SN t1. Thus we need a direct hypothesis about open b v for all values v (or at least related ones).

The logical relation's arrow case handles exactly this: tr (A->B) t = SN t /\ forall u, tr A u -> tr B (app t u). Here the closure under application is a hypothesis, so no need to prove beta preservation syntactically. And the fundamental theorem needs, for T_Abs, to establish `forall u, tr T1 u -> tr T2 (app (abs T1 t1) u)`.

### A cleaner strategy: Inductive "strongly normalizing" closure properties and a direct induction on typing with a "hereditarily SN" relation defined inductively over types.

Another classical approach for CBV STLC: Define *hereditarily normalizing* terms via an inductive family `sn_T` for each type T, where:
- `sn_Bool t := SN t`.
- `sn_{A->B} t := SN t /\ forall v, value v -> sn_A v -> sn_B (app t v)`. Wait, do we need v value? In CBV, app t v steps via beta only if v is a value. But the type-based relation should include all arguments in the relation, and relation members need not be values. Hmm, but terms in the relation of a base type might not be values.

Let's examine what's needed to prove SN of a closed well-typed term via the fundamental theorem. We need the LR to imply SN. The arrow case must include SN t (since tr T t -> SN t is needed for the final theorem). The closure under application is needed for the beta case. If we define closure only for values, does the fundamental theorem's T_App case work? T_App: t1 : A->B, t2 : A, need tr B (app t1 t2). By IH, tr (A->B) t1 gives forall u, tr A u -> tr B (app t1 u) (if closure over all u). If closure only over values v with tr A v, then we'd need t2 to be a value, which it isn't. So we need all u. Unless we define application closure over all u but only need it for u in the relation, and for T_App t2 is any term with tr A t2. So closure must be over all related u. Good, so arrow case: SN t /\ forall u, tr A u -> tr B (app t u).

Now to prove the abstraction case: tr (A->B) (abs A body). We need SN (abs body) (trivial) and forall u, tr A u -> tr B (app (abs body) u).

Given IH: for all x fresh, tr B (open body (fvar x)) under context x:A. We need to derive, for all u with tr A u, tr B (open body u), and then tr B (app (abs body) u).

Since app (abs body) u has reducts: open body u (if value u), and app (abs body) u' for u --> u'. So SN (app (abs body) u) requires SN (open body u) and SN of app (abs body) u' for reducts u'. And tr B (app (abs body) u) for arrow B requires more closure.

So we need the **opening lemma**: from `forall x, tr B (open body (fvar x))` and `tr A u`, derive `tr B (open body u)`. How to prove? Induction on the structure of body seems plausible if we strengthen: The relation `tr T` is closed under opening with related terms. Let's attempt:

Lemma `tr_open`: forall body T, (forall x, tr T (open body (fvar x))) -> forall u, tr_something u -> tr T (open body u)? The hypothesis `forall x` gives us tr at fresh variables. But u is arbitrary. Hmm.

The trick might be that such a strong hypothesis (all fresh variables) is very strong: for t = bvar 0, it says tr T (fvar x) for all x. We need tr T u for any u related at the type of the context. The context info is lost in this statement. But in the actual use, body's typing gives us T and context A. We should do induction on the typing derivation of `open body (fvar x)` rather than semantic hypotheses. I.e., use the *typing substitution lemma*: if `(x:A; Gamma) |- open body (fvar x) : B` then for any u with `Gamma |- u : A`, `Gamma |- open body u : B`. Then apply fundamental theorem to `open body u`. But fundamental theorem needs the IH on the typing derivation of `open body u`, which is derived by substitution—so the induction is on typing derivations, and this substitution lemma is proven independently on typing derivations. That works! The fundamental theorem is proven by induction on the typing derivation of whatever term; in the T_Abs case, the hypothesis is a typing derivation of `open t1 (fvar x)` for all fresh x. We instantiate the IH at x fresh to get semantic relation. Then for u related, we need a *semantic* opening lemma, not a typing one, because the IH gives us semantics for `open t1 (fvar x)`, and we want semantics for `open t1 u`.

Unless... we prove typing substitution and then apply the fundamental theorem to `open t1 u` (derived by typing substitution). But the fundamental theorem's proof is by induction on typing derivations; applying it to `open t1 u`'s derivation is a recursive call on a perhaps-larger derivation, but that's fine if we prove the fundamental theorem by *well-founded induction on the size of typing derivations* or as a lemma with a general statement that is proven by induction and then used recursively? In Coq, we can prove:

`Theorem fundamental : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> forall sigma, ...`

by induction on the derivation. In the T_Abs case, the IH is available for the derivation of `(x|->A; Gamma) |- open t1 (fvar x) : B` (for each fresh x), but not for the derivation of `(Gamma) |- open t1 u : B` obtained via substitution. However, we could prove `fundamental` by strong induction on the *size* of the term t (or derivation), allowing recursive calls on arbitrary derivations of smaller/equal measure? The substituted term `open t1 u` has size roughly |t1| + |u|, while `abs t1`... hmm, sizes could increase.

Alternatively, prove the semantic opening lemma by induction on t1, using the semantic hypotheses and specific structure of tr at T. Let's try to prove it.

**Claim (opening lemma)**: Let A, B be types. Suppose `H : forall x, tr B (open t (fvar x))`. Then for all u with `tr A u`, `tr B (open t u)`? 

As noted this is false for t = bvar 0 unless A = B. But in the actual setting where H comes from a typing derivation, A and B would be related: in the T_Abs case, the context is `x:A` so if t = bvar 0, typing of open t (fvar x) = fvar x at type B under x:A gives A = B.   | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall u, TR T1 u -> TR T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```

**Tait's method for CBV (with values), classical presentation:**

Define for each type A:
- `SN_A` ("strongly normalizing at type A") inductively? or the "hereditary" relation:
  - A value v is in `V_A` (the "value interpretation"):
    - V_Bool = {true, false}
    - V_{A->B} = {λx.t | for all v ∈ V_A, t[v/x] ∈ T_B}
  - A term t is in `T_A` iff t reduces (in some steps, deterministically) to a value in V_A? For SN we need more.

Alternatively define T_A := {t | SN t and every normal form of t is a value in V_A}? Since we need SN anyway for the theorem, this is circular-ish but T_A ⊆ SN is built in; the fundamental theorem shows typed terms ∈ T_A. The beta case requires: if body[v/x] ∈ T_B for all v ∈ V_A, and t ∈ T_A, then app (λx.body) t ∈ T_A. Since t ∈ T_A implies SN t; the reduction of app(λ.body) t includes first reducing t (ST_App2) and beta when t becomes v. We need SN of app(λ.body) t: this follows by induction on SN t? Let's see: app(λ.body) t's reducts: (a) if t is a value v: open body v (which is in T_B ⊆ SN), and app(λ.body) t' where t --> t'. (b) if t not a value: only app(λ.body) t' for t --> t'. So SN (app(λ.body) t) given SN t and (if t value, SN (open body t)) — provable by induction on SN t, but the induction hypothesis is about app(λ.body) t' for all reducts t' of t; also need to know t' ∈ T_A to apply the value case? The value case only needs SN (open body v) for v a *value in V_A*; but induction on SN t gives t' SN, not t' ∈ T_A. However, maybe we can define the induction hypothesis as "forall t' reachable, ...". Hmm, the beta case requires open body v ∈ SN only for values v ∈ V_A. When t steps to a value v, we need to know v ∈ V_A to conclude open body v ∈ SN. Does t ∈ T_A imply its reducts/normal forms are in V_A? If T_A is defined as "SN and normal forms in V_A", then a reduct t' of t might not have its normal forms in V_A? Actually normal forms of t' are normal forms of t (since t -->* t' and t' -->* nf implies t -->* nf), so yes if t ∈ T_A then t' ∈ T_A (if t' SN and nf of t' are nf of t). So T_A is closed under reduction. But SN t' doesn't follow immediately? SN t implies SN t' by definition. OK.

So define T_A := {t | SN t /\ forall t', t -->* t' -> normal_form t' -> V_A t'}. This includes values in V_A? A value v ∈ V_A with SN v: T_A holds. Good. And T_A ⊆ SN.

Fundamental theorem: typed terms ∈ T_T. For T_App: t1 ∈ T_{A->B}, t2 ∈ T_A. Need app t1 t2 ∈ T_B: SN and normal forms in V_B.

- SN (app t1 t2): steps of app t1 t2 are: t1 steps (ST_App1, if lc t2); t2 steps (ST_App2, if v1 value); beta if t1 = λ.body and t2 = v value. Using t1 ∈ T_{A->B} and t2 ∈ T_A. The beta case: if t1 = λ.body and t2 = v, v is a value and v ∈ T_A; since v is a normal form (values are normal? abs/true/false don't step, yes), T_A's normal-form condition gives V_A v; then V_{A->B} (λ.body) gives open body v ∈ T_B ⊆ SN. Other cases: reductions on t1 or t2; by induction on SN t1 and SN t2 with a lexicographic/ mutual argument. This is the classic "SN app" lemma. Formally: prove `SN t1 -> SN t2 -> (forall v, value v -> V_A v -> ...)`? It gets complicated because the beta condition depends on t1 being an abstraction *and* its body.

Hmm, the standard route is to prove:
```
Lemma SN_app : forall t1 t2,
  SN t1 -> SN t2 ->
  (forall v, value v -> t1 = abs ... -> SN (open body v)) -> SN (app t1 t2)
```
Actually, if t1 is an abstraction, SN t1 is trivial, so the hypothesis needed is specifically about its body. We can structure: prove by induction on SN t1 and SN t2 that SN (app t1 t2) under the hypothesis `H : forall v, value v -> SN (open body v)` where t1 = abs ... . But if t1 isn't an abs, no beta. A clean lemma:

`sn_app : forall t1 t2, SN t1 -> SN t2 -> (forall v, value v -> value t1??)`. Hmm.

Alternative: prove SN of `app (abs A body) t` directly by induction on SN t:
- Case t value: need SN (open body t) (hypothesis) and SN of app(abs body) t' for reducts t' — but values have no reducts.
- Case t not value: reducts of app(abs body) t: ST_App2 with t --> t' (needs SN (app abs t') — by IH on SN t'), and ST_AppAbs if t is a value (excluded). Also ST_App1 requires abs --> t1' (impossible).
So `sn_app_abs : SN (open body t) for t value + SN t -> SN (app (abs A body) t)`? Wait, if t is not a value but t has reducts, we need SN (app abs t') for all t'; IH requires SN (open body t') for t' value — but t' might be a value, and our hypothesis gives SN (open body v) only for values v, and t' is a value, so we need the hypothesis for ALL values v with SN v (which is all values; SN v holds for all values). So the lemma:

`Lemma sn_app_abs : forall A body t, SN t -> (forall v, value v -> SN (open body v)) -> SN (app (abs A body) t)`.

Proof: induction on SN t. For each reduct t' of app(abs) t:
- ST_AppAbs: then t is value (hyp: t --> t' means app(abs) t --> open body t); but if t is a value, app(abs) t --> open body t is the only rule; we need SN (open body t) from hypothesis (value t). Also ST_App2 possible if t steps—but t value can't step.
- ST_App2: t --> t'; need SN (app abs t') by IH with SN t'.
- ST_App1: abs --> t1', impossible.
Great, this lemma is provable. Good.

Similarly, `sn_app` for general t1: We want SN (app t1 t2) given SN t1, SN t2, and a hypothesis handling the beta case. The redex case where t1 = abs: if t1 is an abs and t2 is a value, beta. But note t1 may be a term that reduces to an abs. The classic proof by induction on SN t1 then SN t2:

`Lemma sn_app : forall t1 t2, SN t1 -> SN t2 -> (forall A body, t1 = abs A body -> forall v, value v -> SN (open body v)) -> SN (app t1 t2)`? The hypothesis must cover reducts t1' of t1 which are abs. Hmm, if t1 --> t1' and t1' = abs body', we need SN (open body' v) for values v. Not implied by the hypothesis at t1. So the induction needs a stronger hypothesis: for all t1' reachable from t1 (including t1), if t1' = abs A body then for all values v, SN (open body v). This is a "hereditary" condition.

This suggests defining SN in a "hereditary" way that's stable under reduction:

**Define a predicate `sn_abs` or better, define the logical relation at arrow type to include exactly this hereditary condition**:

`tr (A->B) t := SN t /\ forall u, tr A u -> tr B (app t u)`. Then for the T_App case, the beta closure is available by definition: if t1 ∈ tr (A->B) and t2 ∈ tr A, then tr B (app t1 t2) directly. And tr B ⊆ SN. The SN of app t1 t2 comes from tr B, which for arrow includes SN and for Bool includes SN. So T_App is immediate from the definition! Nice. And the T_Abs case is where the work is: for all u with tr A u, tr B (app (abs A body) u), which reduces to tr B (open body u) plus handling the argument stepping.

So the crux remains the opening/substitution: get tr B (open body u) from the fvar-x hypothesis, and then show tr B (app (abs A body) u) from tr B (open body u) and tr A u (with tr A u ⊆ SN u).

Let's focus on proving:

(1) Open lemma: under suitable typing assumptions, forall u, tr A u -> tr B (open body u).

So including locally_closed in tr enables multi-step closure. Let me define:

```
Fixpoint tr (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t =>
      locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall u, tr T1 u -> tr T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```

Recursive calls: tr T1 and tr T2 are subterms; Coq's guard checker should accept. Let me test.

Properties:
- `tr_lc : tr T t -> locally_closed t` (by destruct T; arrow: conjunction; Bool: conjunction).
- `tr_sn : tr T t -> strongly_normalizing t`.
- `tr_app : tr (T1->T2) t -> tr T1 u -> tr T2 (app t u)` (arrow case).
- `lc_app : lc t -> lc u -> lc (app t u)`.
- `lc_open : lc_at 1 body? -> lc u -> lc (open body u)`. Need for the beta case: to establish tr B (app (abs A body) u) requires locally_closed (app (abs A body) u), which needs lc (abs A body) and lc u. But tr A u gives lc u. And lc (abs A body) = lc_at 1 body — do we know it? In the T_Abs case, the typing hypothesis gives lc of `open body (fvar x)` for fresh x (if tr B includes lc), not lc_of abs body directly. Hmm, but we can prove from `forall x, locally_closed (open body (fvar x))` that `lc_at 1 body`? There's a standard lemma: if open body (fvar x) is locally closed for some x not free in body... hmm, lc_at 1 body vs lc_at 0 (open body (fvar x)): opening replaces bvar 0 with fvar x. If body has bvar 1 free, then open body fvar x has bvar 1 free, so lc_at 0 fails. Conversely, if lc_at 0 (open body (fvar x)) and x notin FV body, does lc_at 1 body hold? The bvars of body are ≤ those of open body... bvar 0 is replaced, bvar i>0 unchanged, so lc_at 1 body means bvars < 1, i.e., ≤ 0. Since open body has no bvars, body has only bvar 0. Yes! Because opening only replaces bvar 0 and leaves others. So if lc (open body (fvar x)), then lc_at 1 body. This is getting deep. The open lemma remains central and its type-mismatch issue unresolved. Let's now seriously attack the substitution infrastructure.

### Substitution infrastructure for locally nameless

Let's set up:
- `fv : tm -> list atom` or a predicate `x \in fv t`. Since we need freshness and substitution.
- Free-variable substitution `subst` respecting binders. Capture problem: substituting a non-locally-closed term under a binder. In our use, we can require substituted terms to be locally closed (tr implies lc if we include it). Then no capture: under binders, free-var substitution places terms with no bvars; no capture of bvars. Also, the term being substituted into has binders; fvars are never bound. So simple recursion works:

```
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | ...
  end.
```

If sigma maps to locally closed terms, `subst sigma t` behaves correctly under binders (no shifting needed). But is the result locally closed? `subst sigma (abs T t1)` contains t1 possibly with bvar 0; substituting fvars inside doesn't affect bvars. If t was locally closed (no bvars), the result has no bvars because sigma outputs are lc. Good.

The identity substitution `id_sigma x := fvar x` is lc. So subst id t = t (need to prove, easy by induction).

Now the fundamental theorem with substitutions:

Statement: For all Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> forall sigma, (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> tr T (sigma x)) -> tr T (subst sigma t).

But wait: is this even true if t has free variables not in Gamma? Then subst sigma leaves them as fvar y (since sigma y = fvar y? no, sigma is arbitrary; if Gamma y = None, sigma y can be anything, and the conclusion doesn't constrain it). The fundamental theorem would claim tr T (subst sigma t) where subst sigma replaces ALL free vars using sigma, but the hypothesis only constrains those in Gamma. For free y not in Gamma, sigma y arbitrary could be non-SN, making the conclusion false. Example: Gamma = empty, t = fvar y... but then has_type empty (fvar y) T is impossible. But t could contain fvar y in a way that's typeable? As discussed, with empty Gamma maybe no free vars. In general, typeable terms under Gamma have free vars in dom Gamma. Let's prove a lemma:

`has_type_fv : has_type Gamma t T -> forall x, x \in fv t -> exists T, Gamma x = Some T`. Hmm, is this true? Consider `abs T1 (fvar y)` under Gamma with y in Gamma. Fine. The T_Abs rule opens with fresh x; fv of open t1 fvar x = (fv t1 \ {x}) ∪ {x}. The IH gives all these in dom (x|->T1;Gamma). For y ∈ fv t1, y ∈ dom, so Gamma y = Some T or y = x. If y = x, then Gamma y might be None, but t = abs T1 t1 with free var x where x was chosen fresh w.r.t. L but maybe not fresh w.r.t. fv t1! In the T_Abs rule, x must be not in L only. But L is arbitrary, chosen by the rule's user. Hmm, the typing derivation's T_Abs node has a specific L. If t1 has free var y and y ∉ L, then choosing x = y is allowed by "forall x, x ∉ L", and then open t1 fvar y has free y, typed under y:T1;Gamma where Gamma y might be None — the T_Var rule requires the full context to map y to Some T, i.e., `update Gamma y T1 y = Some T1` — true regardless of Gamma! Because update gives Some T1 at y. So open t1 fvar y is typed. But then the abstraction `abs T1 t1` has free y (in t1) while the context Gamma has y ↦ None. Is such a derivation possible? Let's construct: Gamma = empty. t = `abs Bool (fvar y)` where y is some atom, say 0. T_Abs with L = []? Then need forall x, has_type (x|->Bool; empty) (open (fvar y) (fvar x)) Bool. open (fvar y) (fvar x) = fvar y (opening doesn't touch fvars). So need forall x, has_type (x|->Bool;empty) (fvar y) Bool. For x = y: context y|->Bool;empty maps y to Some Bool, so T_Var works. For x ≠ y: context x|->Bool;empty maps y to None, so fails. Since the hypothesis is forall x ∉ L = all x, it fails for x ≠ y. So the derivation doesn't exist. Good.

But note the T_Abs rule as given quantifies over a specific L; to derive a useful inversion we can choose x fresh w.r.t. L ∪ fv(t1) ∪ relevant stuff. The rule's hypothesis is strong enough.

So indeed typeable terms have fv ⊆ dom Gamma. Let's prove:

`Lemma has_type_fv_dom : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> forall x, x \in fv t -> Gamma x <> None.` (or exists T, Gamma x = Some T).

Proof by induction on the typing derivation.
- T_Var: t = fvar x, fv = {x}; hypothesis Gamma x = Some T.
- T_Abs: t = abs T1 t1; fv t1 = fv t. Need forall x ∈ fv t1, Gamma x = Some. Take x ∈ fv t1. Choose a fresh variable z ∉ L ∪ fv t1 (and maybe z ≠ x). Hmm, but the induction hypothesis applies to the derivation of `open t1 (fvar z)` under `update Gamma z T1`. We need to relate fv of that term to fv t1. Opening replaces bvar 0 with fvar z; free vars of t1 remain, plus possibly z if bvar 0 occurs. So fv(open t1 fvar z) = fv(t1) ∪ {z} (if bvar 0 occurs) or fv(t1) (if not; actually either way it's fv(t1) ∪ {z} because opening bvar 0 inserts fvar z; if bvar 0 doesn't occur, z isn't inserted, so fv = fv t1). Anyway x ∈ fv t1 ⊆ fv(open t1 fvar z). IH gives update Gamma z T1 x ≠ None. Since z ≠ x (choose fresh), update Gamma z T1 x = Gamma x, so Gamma x ≠ None. Need to know fv(open t1 fvar z) ⊇ fv t1: yes, opening doesn't remove free vars. Need a lemma.
- T_App: IH on both.
- T_True/False: no free vars.

But for constructing the "semantic environment" we need for each x in fv t, tr T (sigma x) for the appropriate T = Gamma x. So the statement env_sem is over all x with Gamma x = Some T; sigma x could be chosen as fvar x for variables not in dom. Since subst sigma t only touches fv t ⊆ dom, we're fine.

Now the inductive proof of the fundamental theorem. Let's attempt:

```
Theorem fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma,
    (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> tr T (sigma x)) ->
    tr T (subst sigma t).
```

Induction on the typing derivation.

- T_Var: t = fvar x, Gamma x = Some T. subst sigma (fvar x) = sigma x. Need tr T (sigma x): apply H_env.

- T_True/False: subst sigma true = true. Need tr Bool true: lc true and SN true. SN true: SN_intro with no steps (inversion on step with true as source: no rule). Good.

- T_App: t = app t1 t2, T = T2, has_type Gamma t1 (T1->T2), has_type Gamma t2 T1. IH1: forall sigma, env -> tr (T1->T2) (subst sigma t1). IH2: ... tr T1 (subst sigma t2). Goal tr T2 (subst sigma (app t1 t2)) = tr T2 (app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)). Apply tr_app with the IH results. 

- T_Abs: t = abs T1 t1, T = T1->T2. Goal: tr (T1->T2) (subst sigma (abs T1 t1)) = tr (T1->T2) (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)). Need:
  (a) lc (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)), i.e., lc_at 1 (subst sigma t1).
  (b) SN (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)): trivial (no steps from abs), regardless.
  (c) forall u, tr T1 u -> tr T2 (app (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) u).

For (c), we need tr T2 (app (abs T1 body') u) where body' = subst sigma t1. Using the typing hypothesis: forall x ∉ L, has_type (x|->T1; Gamma) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2. We want to apply the IH to this typing derivation with substitution sigma' = [x := u] + sigma (i.e., sigma' x = u, sigma' y = sigma y for y ≠ x). Then IH gives:
  tr T2 (subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)))  provided env_sem (x|->T1;Gamma) sigma'.
  
And env_sem sigma' for update Gamma x T1: for y with update Gamma x T1 y = Some T, need tr T (sigma' y). If y = x: T = T1, sigma' y = u, and we have tr T1 u. If y ≠ x: Gamma y = Some T, sigma' y = sigma y, use env_sem sigma.

Then subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) should equal open (subst sigma t1) u = open body' u, provided x is fresh (not free in t1, x ∉ L perhaps) and sigma' agrees. Let's verify:
  open t1 (fvar x) replaces bvar 0 by fvar x.
  subst sigma' on that: replaces free vars (those of t1, plus x) by sigma'. Result = open t1 u with free vars of t1 replaced by sigma? Wait after opening with fvar x, bvar 0 is gone (replaced by fvar x), so the result has no bvar 0 at top level except within t1's structure? No: open_rec replaces ALL bvars equal to 0 in t1? `open_rec k u t` recurses; under abs it increments k. `open t u = open_rec 0 u t` replaces bvar 0 throughout t, except under binders where k increments so bvar 0 inside a binder refers to that binder and is not replaced. So open t1 (fvar x) is t1 with the *outermost* bvar 0 replaced by fvar x. Then subst sigma' replaces free vars including x. Since x is chosen fresh (not free in t1), the only occurrence of fvar x is the inserted one, replaced by u. The other free vars y of t1 (in the opened term) get sigma'(y) = sigma(y) (since y ≠ x). Bound vars are untouched by subst. So the result = open (subst sigma t1) u? Let's see: subst sigma t1 replaces free vars y of t1 by sigma y, leaves bvar 0. Then open with u replaces bvar 0 by u. Compare: open then subst sigma': free vars of opened = fv(t1) ∪ {x}; replace x by u, y by sigma y; bvar 0 occurrences outside binders were replaced by fvar x and then u; bvar 0 under binders remains. subst sigma t1 then open u: free vars replaced, bvar 0 (outermost) replaced by u (including those under binders? open_rec under binders increments k, so bvar 0 under an inner binder is not replaced — correct). So yes, they match, *provided* opening and free-var substitution commute appropriately, and x is fresh w.r.t. fv t1 while sigma' agrees with sigma on fv t1.

The needed syntactic lemma:
`subst_open : forall t1 u sigma x, x \notin fv t1 -> (forall y, y ≠ x -> sigma' y = sigma y) -> sigma' x = u -> subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma t1) u.`
Or simpler: define sigma' = fun y => if eqb y x then u else sigma y; need x fresh in t1 and maybe sigma's outputs locally closed for capture? Let's see capture: subst sigma t1 under binders: sigma y might have bvars, which would get captured by t1's binders. But we require sigma y locally closed (from env_sem + tr implies lc). Yes, if tr T (sigma y) implies lc (sigma y), then no bvars, no capture. But in the syntactic lemma, we need lc of sigma's outputs to ensure subst behaves as expected? Actually subst as defined doesn't shift, so it just inserts sigma y under binders. If sigma y has bvars, they'd be captured. For the equality `subst sigma' (open t1 fvar x) = open (subst sigma t1) u` we need to be careful: if sigma y has bvars and appears under a binder in both sides, both sides would... hmm, both sides would have the same capture, since both substitute at the same binder depth. Actually the equality might hold regardless of capture, because opening only replaces bvar 0 and does not affect bvars from sigma. Let's verify by induction, but it's plausible that the equality holds syntactically for any sigma, u. The freshness of x is crucial to avoid replacing unintended fvar x's. But if x occurs in sigma y's outputs? Since sigma' is applied at the top-level of the opened term; inside, subst sigma' recurses into subterms, including any term sigma' y inserted? NO: subst sigma' (tm_fvar y) = sigma' y; it does NOT recurse into sigma' y. So x occurring inside sigma y won't be replaced by u. Good. So the equality likely holds with just x ∉ fv t1.

Let's step back and consider a different definition of the arrow candidate that makes these proofs manageable, perhaps the standard "reducibility candidate" definition with conditions:

A set C of terms is a candidate for type A if:
1. C ⊆ SN.
2. If t ∈ C and t --> t', then t' ∈ C.
3. If t is neutral (not a value? in CBV, neutral means not an abstraction?) and all its one-step reducts are in C, then t ∈ C.
4. If t ∈ C then ...?

In Girard's proof for call-by-name, candidates are sets of SN terms closed under reduction and containing neutral terms whose reducts are in C. For CBV, the notion of neutral is different: a term is neutral if it's not an abstraction? And values of function type are not neutral. The candidate for function type consists of terms t such that (i) t is SN, (ii) for all u ∈ C_A, app t u ∈ C_B? And neutral terms: fvar x is neutral; if all reducts of a neutral term are in C then the term is in C. This handles fvar.

But our tr definition (Fixpoint) is essentially this, with the arrow closure over all related u. The problem is proving the abstraction case's arrow closure: forall u related, tr B (app (abs body) u). In Girard's proof, one shows: if for all v ∈ V_A (values), body[v] ∈ C_B, then λ.body ∈ C_{A->B}. The term λ.body is SN (values are SN). For the arrow closure: app (λ.body) u ∈ C_B for u ∈ C_A. One proves by induction on the "candidate" C_B: app (λ.body) u reduces to body[u] only if u is a value; if u is not a value, u reduces and we use induction on SN u. And app(λ.body)u' for u --> u' with u' ∈ C_A by closure. Inductive tr : ty -> tm -> Prop :=

| tr_abs : forall A B body,

| tr_var : forall A x, tr A (fvar x)   (* or neutral terms *)
```

### Approach: Translate to call-by-name / use the standard "strong normalization via reducibility" but exploit determinism and closedness

Since CBV STLC is deterministic, and the target is a *closed* term t with empty context, maybe we can prove SN via a *big-step evaluation* semantics and a logical relation on values, plus a "determinism + progress" argument that all small steps eventually reach a value and that values are normal. But SN requires all reduction paths finite, not just the deterministic one. However, the step relation might be deterministic for locally closed terms! Let's check: For a locally closed term, can there be two different rules applying? 
- app (abs A body) v with v value: ST_AppAbs applies (requires lc abs, value v). ST_App1 applies if abs --> abs' — no. ST_App2 applies if value abs (yes) and v --> v' — no since v value. So unique.
- app t1 t2 with t1 not a value and t1 --> t1': ST_App1 applies. ST_AppAbs needs t1 = abs, no. ST_App2 needs value t1, no. Unique.
- app t1 t2 with t1 value not abs? t1 = true/false. If t2 --> t2', ST_App2 applies; ST_App1 needs true --> t1', no. Unique. But if t2 doesn't step, no rule applies (stuck).
- app (abs) t2 with t2 not value: ST_App2 applies if t2 --> t2'; ST_AppAbs needs value t2, no; ST_App1 needs abs step, no. Unique.
So yes, step is deterministic on all terms? The side conditions don't break uniqueness because the cases are disjoint by shape/value. Actually consider app t1 t2 where t1 is an abs and t2 is not a value but t2 steps: ST_App2 only. Fine. Consider a term with both ST_App1 and ST_App2 possible: requires t1 steps and t1 is a value and t2 steps. A value that steps? Values don't step. So disjoint. **Step is deterministic.**

Then SN t ↔ no infinite reduction path. With determinism, all reduction paths from t are prefixes of the unique maximal path. So SN t iff there is no infinite path iff the unique path terminates in a normal form. So strong normalization = weak normalization! For deterministic systems. 

So we can prove: every closed well-typed term reduces to a normal form (value). We could use a *big-step* semantics (evaluation) and prove every typed term evaluates, then show that big-step evaluation implies no infinite small-step path (by a "determinacy/coherence of big and small step" argument). Hmm, still need to relate big-step to all small-step paths.

Alternatively, prove SN by induction on the *unique* reduction length? We don't know the length.

Hmm, but conceptually with determinism, proving `exists v, t -->* v /\ value v` (weak normalization) suffices, given determinism + "normal forms are values" + "values don't step" + the fact that any infinite small-step sequence would contradict reaching a normal form... Actually if t -->* v with v normal and step deterministic, any reduction path from t is a prefix of the path to v? Not necessarily: determinism says at each step there's at most one successor, and the path to v is a sequence; any other path must follow the same unique successor sequence, so it's a prefix of the path to v (if v is reached, the path ends at v since v is normal). Hence every path is finite. Good.

```
Then define the logical relation on *values* and show every typed term evaluates to a value in the relation. ```
tr (A->B) t := lc t /\ SN t /\ (forall t', t -->* t' -> forall u, tr A u -> tr B (app t' u))

  Hbody v := lc(open body v) /\ SN(open body v) /\ (forall s, open body v -->* s -> forall w, tr A w -> tr B (app s w)).

    Analyze the multi-step derivation: `multi` is defined inductively: multi_refl or multi_step R x y z. It's a sequence of steps. We can prove a lemma by induction on the multi-step derivation:
    
    Claim: for all n-th step prefixes... Let's characterize reductions from app(abs body) u. The term's head is the abstraction applied to u. Steps:
    - ST_App2: app(abs body) u --> app(abs body) u' when value (abs body) [true] and u --> u'. Requires u --> u'.
    - ST_AppAbs: app(abs body) u --> open body u when value u.

So the first case persists: we need tr B (app (app (abs body) u*) w) for u* reachable. Note that app (abs body) u* is stuck if u* is a normal form non-value. But u* might still step? In the multi-step prefix ending at app(abs body)u*, u* might be non-normal, and further steps exist. But for the current t', we need the closure property at t' = app(abs body)u*, which includes all further reducts... 

I think the right statement to prove is by induction on SN u, the following:

**Claim C(u)**: lc(abs body) -> (forall v, tr T1 v -> tr T2 (open body v)) -> tr T1 u -> tr T2 (app (abs body) u).

And the arrow case needs a stronger claim. Let's see if we can prove C(u) by induction on SN u directly, with T2 fixed, doing induction on T2 *inside*? The problematic arrow case: tr B (app (app(abs body) u) w) for related w. Perhaps we can prove this as a corollary of C at a *different type*: define body₂ := ... such that app (app (abs body) u) w = app (abs ?) ? No.

Alternative: prove the general lemma at the level of SN:

**Lemma sn_app_general**: For all t1 t2, SN t1 -> SN t2 -> (forall v, value v -> SN (app t1 v)?)... 

Hmm, wait. Maybe we can prove tr B (app t' w) using the *SN* of the relevant terms plus the definition of tr, where the arrow closure can be established by induction on SN of the argument w or something? Let's look at the structure of tr B for B = B1 -> B2: it requires closure under application with related arguments. For the term app (app abs u) w, applying again to w2 gives app (app (app abs u) w) w2. The head is still an abs applied to multiple args. In general, these "spine" terms with an abstraction head are all reducible: if u is a value and w is a value... but if not, they are stuck or step on arguments.

Honestly, the situation suggests we need a substantially different formulation, e.g., **logical relation on values + evaluation**, which avoids needing tr of "partially applied abstractions" at arbitrary types. Let me think about the big-step approach.

### Big-step / value-based logical relation

Define CBV evaluation relation (deterministic, total on well-typed terms):

```
Inductive eval : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
| eval_val : forall v, value v -> eval v v
| eval_app : forall t1 t2 A body v1 v2 v,
    eval t1 (tm_abs A body) ->
    eval t2 v2 ->
    value v2 ->           (* maybe implied? *)
    eval (open body v2) v ->
    eval (app t1 t2) v
```

Hmm, or simpler: define eval as the transitive closure of step to a normal form? We can work with `t -->* v /\ value v` directly. Let's define WN(t) := exists v, t -->* v /\ value v.

Define the value interpretation `V T v` (v a value):
- V Bool v := v = true \/ v = false.
- V (A->B) v := exists A' body, v = abs A' body /\ A' = A /\ forall w, value w -> V A w -> exists v', open body w -->* v' /\ V B v'.
Then define the "term interpretation" `E T t := exists v, t -->* v /\ V T v`.
(Maybe with steps).

Then strong normalization: if t -->* v and v is a value, and the relation "t -->* v" is deterministic... To conclude SN t, we need that all reduction sequences from t end at v. Determinism+normal value v gives that. But proving "normal form = value" needs: any locally closed well-typed-ish term that is a normal form is a value. Hmm, but ill-typed terms can be stuck non-values like app true true. For a well-typed term we'd have progress: either value or steps. And `t -->* v` with v value normal means t is weakly normalizing; with determinism, any maximal path from t must be the unique one ending at v (since deterministic, the successor of each reachable term is unique; an infinite path would have to follow the same terms and never reach v, but the finite path to v shows at each of those terms the unique successor; so any path reaches v and stops). Yes.

Proving progress for well-typed closed terms requires typing inversion and locally-closedness. Doable but adds work.

The logical relation E/V: fundamental theorem: every typed term t (in context Gamma, under substitution sigma) satisfies E T (subst sigma t). This still needs the substitution/opening machinery. For T_Abs: we need E (A->B) (abs A body). It suffices to show value (abs A body) and V (A->B) (abs A body). V requires forall value w in V A, exists v', open body w -->* v' /\ V B v'. We know for all fresh x, E B (open body (fvar x)) (from IH on typing), which gives exists v', open body (fvar x) -->* v' /\ V B v'. But we need it for a value w, not fvar x. Again substitution.

Unless... we can prove that E B (open body w) for arbitrary w by a semantic substitution lemma on evaluations. Hmm.

### Alternative: use the cofinite quantification cleverly with a *renaming* of free variables to the argument term?

Wait. Let's reconsider the T_Abs typing rule. Its hypothesis quantifies over all fresh x. In the fundamental theorem, the induction hypothesis is: for all x fresh, for all sigma env, tr B (subst sigma (open t1 (fvar x))). We want tr B (open (subst sigma t1) u). We set sigma = [x ↦ u] + sigma_0 and use the syntactic identity subst [x↦u]+sigma_0 (open t1 fvar x) = open (subst sigma_0 t1) u. This gives us tr B (open body u) for ANY related u, as I described. So the opening lemma is solved by the IH + substitution identity. 

Thus in the T_Abs case we DO get Hbody : forall u, tr T1 u -> tr T2 (open body u) for all related u. The remaining problem is purely: from tr T2 (open body u) and tr T1 u, prove tr T2 (app (abs T1 body) u). This is the "beta expansion" lemma. It's independent of typing! Good. Let's focus on it.

**Beta expansion lemma**: For all types T1, T2, body, u:
`tr T2 (open body u) -> tr T1 u -> locally_closed (abs T1 body) -> tr T2 (app (abs T1 body) u)`?

Wait, from Hbody (all related u) we have tr T2 (open body u). But is this lemma true for arbitrary u related and tr T2 (open body u)? For a value v related at T1, we need SN (open body v); we have it if we have tr T2 (open body v) for all related v, which we do (Hbody). So yes.

For T2 = A->B: need tr (A->B) (app (abs body) u). Requires forall w, tr A w -> tr B (app (app abs u) w).

Can we derive this from tr (A->B) (open body u) and tr T1 u? tr (A->B) (open body u) gives forall w, tr A w -> tr B (app (open body u) w). So we need to go from tr B (app (open body u) w) to tr B (app (app abs u) w). Hmm, this is a backward-expansion property at type B, which we can prove maybe by a separate induction:

**Claim (backward closure under abs-expansion)**: For all types T, body, u, w:
`tr T (app (open body u) w) -> tr T1 u -> lc(abs T1 body) -> tr T (app (app (abs T1 body) u) w)`?

Hmm, this is a weird property. Let's test with T = Bool: need SN(app(app abs u) w) given SN(app(open body u) w), tr T1 u, lc abs. **Generalized beta expansion lemma**:
For all T, u, and terms of the form `app (abs T1 body) u`, tr T (app (abs T1 body) u) given:
- lc (abs T1 body), tr T1 u, and
- for all related v, tr T (open body v)? 

Hmm, the T2=Bool proof needs SN(open body v) for related v; the T2=A->B case needs the closure, which recursively needs something stronger.

What if we generalize to **n-ary applications of an abstraction**? The key property of `app (abs body) u`: its reducts. Maybe prove:

**Claim**: For all T, body, and a list/stack of related arguments, the iterated application `app (... (app (abs T1 body) u1) ... ) un` is in tr T provided the arguments are related and T matches. Actually, here's a cleaner idea: **prove that tr T (app (abs T1 body) u) directly by well-founded induction on (T, u) where u ranges over SN terms**, and in the arrow case, the subgoal `tr B (app (app abs u) w)` can be proven by applying the *lemma recursively at type B with a different "abstraction"*: note `app (app (abs body) u) w` can be seen as the result of applying the abstraction `abs T1 body` to u and then w. For arrow T: tr T s = lc s ∧ SN s ∧ forall u, tr A u -> tr B (app s u). Given s --> s' and tr T s'. SN s from SN s' (deterministic: any path from s starts with this step to s', so SN s). lc s given. Closure: need forall u related, tr B (app s u). Since s --> s', app s u --> app s' u (ST_App1, needs lc u which tr A u provides). By induction on the type B, backward closure at B: tr B (app s' u) (from tr T s' closure) and app s u --> app s' u gives tr B (app s u). This works if we prove backward closure by induction on T, using determinism for SN!

So we get: **backward closure under step**, provided we have locally_closed s. Nice. This is a standard property of deterministic systems.

Then the beta expansion lemma becomes easy at the top level when u is a value: app (abs body) u --> open body u; tr T2 (open body u) and lc (app abs u) gives tr T2 (app abs u) by backward closure. So the stuck case is only for terms like `app abs u` with u stuck non-value: they are "neutral" terms (not values), and applications of a neutral term to anything are stuck (since the head is a neutral application, which is not a value, and can't step because the whole spine is neutral). - Bool: lc, SN.

We need lc of stuck t: given. In our scenario, t = app (abs body) u where u is stuck normal and lc u, lc abs body gives lc t. Good.

So the plan for beta expansion:
**Lemma `tr_abs_app`** (prove by induction on SN u):

#### tr_step (forward closure)

`Lemma tr_step : forall T t t', tr T t -> t --> t' -> tr T t'.`
Induction on T.
- T = Bool: tr t = lc t ∧ SN t. Need lc t' (lc_step) and SN t' (SN_step).
- T = Arrow A B: tr t = lc t ∧ SN t ∧ (forall u, tr A u -> tr B (app t u)).
  - lc t': lc_step.
  - SN t': SN_step.
  - forall u, tr A u -> tr B (app t' u): have tr B (app t u). We have app t u --> app t' u by ST_App1 with locally_closed u (tr_lc at A). Then apply IH at B: tr B (app t u) -> app t u --> app t' u -> tr B (app t' u). Note IH at type B is available since B is a subterm of A -> B. But careful with Coq's induction on T: when doing `induction T`, the arrow case has T = Ty_Arrow T1 T2, and IH for T1 and T2. The recursive call tr T2 ... is on T2. Good.
  
  Wait: `tr T t` is a Fixpoint, not an inductive; destructing T gives tr defined. `induction T` with the goal forall t t', tr T t -> ... will give IH for T1 and T2 as `forall t t', tr T1 t -> ...`. Good.

#### lc_step

`Lemma lc_step : forall t t', locally_closed t -> t --> t' -> locally_closed t'.`
Induction on the step derivation (which is structurally fine).
- ST_AppAbs: lc(app (abs A body) v) = lc_app: lc(abs A body) ∧ lc v. lc(abs A body) = lc_at 1 body. Need lc(open body v) = lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 v body). Use lc_at_open with k=0: need lc_at 1 body and lc_at 0 v. Have.
- ST_App1: lc(app t1 t2): lc t1, lc t2. Step t1 --> t1' with IH: lc t1'. Need lc(app t1' t2): lc_app. Good.
- ST_App2: lc v1, lc t2; step t2 --> t2'; IH lc t2'; lc(app v1 t2'). Good.

Need `lc_at_open : forall k body v, lc_at (S k) body -> lc_at k v -> lc_at k (open_rec k v body)` and `lc_at_weaken : lc_at k t -> lc_at (S k) t` and `lc_app`.

Also useful: value -> locally_closed:
`value_lc : value v -> locally_closed v`.
- v_abs requires lc directly.
- v_true/v_false: lc_true/lc_false.

#### step determinism

`Lemma step_det : forall t t1 t2, t --> t1 -> t --> t2 -> t1 = t2.`
Proof by induction on the first step derivation, then inversion on the second.
- ST_AppAbs: t = app (abs A body) v with value v, t1 = open body v. Inversion on second step:
  - ST_AppAbs with same abs and v': equal because t = app (abs A body) v.
  - ST_App1: t = app t1' t2 with t1' --> t1''; but t1' = abs A body, which doesn't step; inversion on the step of abs gives contradiction.
  - ST_App2: t = app v1 t2 with value v1 and t2 --> t2'; here v1 = abs A body and t2 = v. But v is a value, and a value doesn't step; contradiction. Need `value_no_step : value v -> ~ (v --> v')`.
- ST_App1: t = app a b, a --> a', t1 = app a' b. Inversion on second:
  - ST_AppAbs: requires t's function be an abs; a --> a' impossible if a is abs? Actually inversion gives a = abs ..., and then a --> a' contradicts value_no_step (abs is a value). 
  - ST_App1: a --> a'' and result app a'' b; use IH on a --> a' and a --> a'' to get a' = a''. Then equal.
  - ST_App2: requires a value; contradiction with a --> a'.
- ST_App2: t = app v1 b with value v1, b --> b', t1 = app v1 b'. Inversion on second:
  - ST_AppAbs: requires the function be an abs; v1 is a value; if v1 = abs, then the second rule's body... Hmm, this is a genuine overlap! t = app (abs A body) b with value (abs A body) and b --> b'. The first step is ST_App2 giving app (abs A body) b'. The second step ST_AppAbs giving open body b — requires value b. But b --> b' means b is not a value. Contradiction: a value cannot step, so b is not a value; ST_AppAbs requires value b. Contradiction.
  - ST_App1: requires v1 --> something; value_no_step contradiction.
  - ST_App2: b --> b'' and result app v1 b''; IH.
So determinism holds. Good. Also need `value_no_step : value v -> forall v', ~ (v --> v')`: inversion on step and value.

Actually, simpler: prove `step_det` by inversion on both and use `value_no_step` and `abs_no_step`.

#### SN facts

`SN_step : SN t -> t --> t' -> SN t'` — inversion on SN t.

`SN_from_unique_step`: if s --> s' and SN s', and ... hmm, for backward closure we want SN s from SN s' when s has at most one step. Precisely: if `forall x, s --> x -> x = s'` (s has unique reduct s') and SN s', then SN s. Proof: SN_intro; for each reduct x of s, x = s', so SN x.

More useful: with determinism, if s --> s' and SN s' then SN s. Because any reduct x of s equals s' by determinism (step_det with the given step). Yes.

So `SN_of_step_back : s --> s' -> SN s' -> SN s`? Wait, this would say SN s' implies SN s whenever s steps to s'. With determinism, yes: any reduct of s is s' (unique), which is SN, so SN s. But careful: SN_intro requires all reducts of s to be SN; they're all s', and SN s' holds. So yes! That's a general fact for deterministic step. Nice, this makes backward closure of SN trivial.

Hmm, but wait: that means SN is backward closed under step: s --> s' and SN s' implies SN s. Combined with forward closure, SN s ↔ SN s' when s steps to s'. Great. Is that right? In a deterministic system, yes: if s steps to s', then all paths from s go through s' and continue; so SN s iff SN s'. Correct.

So `SN_back : forall s s', s --> s' -> SN s' -> SN s` via SN_intro + step_det.

Wait, careful: SN_intro's premise is forall t', s --> t' -> SN t'. For each such t', step_det gives t' = s', so SN t'. Good.

#### tr_back_step

`Lemma tr_back_step : forall T s s', tr T s' -> s --> s' -> locally_closed s -> tr T s.`
Induction on T.
- Bool: lc s, SN s from SN_back with step and SN s'.
- Arrow A B: lc s; SN s from SN_back; forall u, tr A u -> tr B (app s u).
  Have tr B (app s' u) from tr T s' (arrow part). And app s u --> app s' u via ST_App1, requiring locally_closed u (tr_lc at A). Apply IH at B (subterm) with lc(app s u) (lc_app of lc s and lc u), giving tr B (app s u). 
  Need IH at B: `forall s s', tr B s' -> s --> s' -> lc s -> tr B s`. Yes, available.
  
  Hmm, but there's a subtlety: the app s u step ST_App1 with the given s --> s' and lc u. Yes.

Great.

#### tr_stuck

Define `stuck t := locally_closed t /\ (forall t', ~ (t --> t')) /\ ~ value t`? Actually we need "not a value" only to ensure app t w is not a value? Wait, app t w is never a value regardless. For the induction's closure: tr B (app t w) requires tr (B1->B2) (app t w), which needs app t w stuck: no steps and lc. No steps of app t w: if t has no steps and t is not a value, then ST_AppAbs (needs t = abs, i.e., value) and ST_App1 (needs t step) and ST_App2 (needs value t) all fail. So "not value" is needed for ST_AppAbs/ST_App2? ST_App2 requires `value t`; if t is not a value, fails. ST_AppAbs requires `tm_app ...` pattern: t must be an abs, which is a value, contradiction. So yes, need `~ value t`.

Lemma: `tr_stuck : forall T t, locally_closed t -> (forall t', ~ (t --> t')) -> ~ value t -> tr T t.`
Induction on T:
- Bool: lc, SN (SN_intro with no steps).
- Arrow A B: lc, SN, and forall u, tr A u -> tr B (app t u).
  Need tr B (app t u): apply IH at B with:
  - lc(app t u): lc t, lc u (tr_lc A u).
  - no steps from app t u: inversion; cases: ST_AppAbs needs t = abs (value t) contradiction; ST_App1 needs t --> t' contradiction; ST_App2 needs value t contradiction.
  - not value (app t u): constructor disjoint.
  Good. IH at B available.

Let me re-examine the beta expansion lemma proof with these tools:

```
Lemma tr_abs_app : forall T1 T2 body u,
  locally_closed (tm_abs T1 body) ->
  (forall v, tr T1 v -> tr T2 (open body v)) ->
  tr T1 u ->
  tr T2 (tm_app (tm_abs T1 body) u).
Proof.
  intros T1 T2 body u Hlc Hbody Htr.
  (* induction on SN u *)
  assert (Hsn : strongly_normalizing u) by apply tr_sn in Htr.
  remember Hsn as Hsn'.  (* for induction *)
  revert T2 Hbody Htr.
  induction Hsn as [u Hu_sn IH].
  intros T2 Hbody Htr.
  ...
```

Hmm, careful with the induction principle: `induction Hsn` where Hsn : strongly_normalizing u. The motive would be `fun u => forall T2 body..., ...` but body and T1 fixed? T2 should be generalized because...? Actually Hbody is at T2; but the induction hypothesis for reducts u' of u is at the same T2? Let's see the proof:

We want P(u) := forall T2 body (with T1 fixed), lc(abs T1 body) -> (forall v, tr T1 v -> tr T2 (open body v)) -> tr T1 u -> tr T2 (app (abs T1 body) u).

In the step case (u has reduct u'): need P(u') to conclude tr T2 (app abs u'). P(u') at the same T2/body: need (forall v, tr T1 v -> tr T2 (open body v)) — same Hbody; and tr T1 u' — from tr_step. So same T2 works. No need to generalize T2 over the induction. But could the proof need P(u') at a different T2? In the case analysis:
- value case: Hbody u Htr gives tr T2 (open body u); backward step.
- non-value with reduct u': IH at u' with same T2, Hbody, tr_step Htr.
- stuck: tr_stuck at T2.
So T2 fixed is fine. But in Coq, if we do `induction Hsn` with T2 in the context, we need to `revert` the things that depend on u before induction: Htr : tr T1 u depends on u; Hbody doesn't depend on u (it's `forall v, tr T1 v -> ...`). Hsn depends on u. So `remember`/`revert Htr`; but the induction principle's motive must also include T2 (not dependent on u, so no need to revert, but the induction hypothesis would be restricted to the same T2 if T2 stays in context—fine, we don't need other T2).

Wait, actually, in the step case we might need the induction hypothesis at the same T2. Fine. However, to phrase the goal properly, the motive P must not mention u in a way that prevents generalization of Htr. Use:

```

Proof.

  assert (Hsn : SN u) by (apply tr_sn in Htr; exact Htr).
  revert Htr.

#### tr_lc, tr_sn, tr_app

- tr_lc : tr T t -> lc t. By destruct/induction on T; each clause is a conjunction.
- tr_sn : tr T t -> SN t. Same.
- tr_app : tr (T1->T2) t -> tr T1 u -> tr T2 (app t u). For arrow, tr is lc ∧ SN ∧ closure; apply closure.
  Note: tr (T1->T2) t unfolds definitionally. But careful: `tr` is a Fixpoint; `tr (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) t` should reduce to `lc t /\ SN t /\ forall u, tr T1 u -> tr T2 (app t u)`. Use `simpl` or `unfold tr; fold tr`. Since tr is a Fixpoint on the type, `simpl (tr (T1->T2) t)` should unfold.
- tr_sn for arrow: the SN component.
- tr_lc for arrow: lc component.

Let me now write the syntactic substitution lemmas:

1. `subst` definition (capture-avoiding? we rely on lc inputs).
```
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.
```

2. `subst_id : subst (fun x => tm_fvar x) t = t` by induction on t.

3. `subst_open_fvar` identity:
`forall t u sigma x, x ∉ fv t -> subst (update_sigma sigma x u) (open t (tm_fvar x)) = open (subst sigma t) u`
where update_sigma sigma x u := fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y. But careful: `open` uses open_rec which replaces bvar 0 by (tm_fvar x) at the top; under binders k increments. subst then replaces free vars. Let's prove by induction on t generalizing... hmm, open under binders changes k; subst doesn't care. The equality at the top level (k=0). Under binders, open_rec (S k) with fvar x vs open_rec 0? Let's think: open t (fvar x) = open_rec 0 (fvar x) t. subst acts on all subterms including under binders. The RHS open (subst sigma t) u = open_rec 0 u (subst sigma t).

Proof by induction on t:
- bvar i: open_rec 0 fvarx (bvar i) = if i =? 0 then fvar x else bvar i. 
  LHS: if i=0 then subst ... (fvar x) = update_sigma sigma x u x = u (since eqb x x true); else subst (bvar i) = bvar i.
  RHS: open_rec 0 u (subst sigma (bvar i)) = open_rec 0 u (bvar i) = if i=0 then u else bvar i. Match.

On the LHS, opening happens first (fvar y is not affected), then subst replaces y by sigma y without opening it. So the two differ when sigma y contains bvar 0. 

Hmm! So we need sigma outputs to be locally closed (no bvars) to make the equality hold, OR we need to shift. So the substitution identity lemma will require `forall y, locally_closed (sigma y)` (or at least relevant ones). Let's include it.

So the lemma:
```
Lemma subst_open_rec : forall sigma sigma' x u t k,
  (forall y, locally_closed (sigma y)) ->
  sigma' x = u ->
  (forall y, y <> x -> sigma' y = sigma y) ->
  x ∉ fv t ->
  subst sigma' (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t) = open_rec k u (subst sigma t).
```
Or specialize sigma' = fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y. Then sigma' x = u and for y ≠ x, sigma' y = sigma y. Let's use the specialized version for simplicity:
`subst_open_rec : forall sigma x u t k, (forall y, locally_closed (sigma y)) -> locally_closed u -> x ∉ fv t -> subst (extend x u sigma) (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t) = open_rec k u (subst sigma t)`
where `extend x u sigma y := if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y`.

Then the final theorem:
```
Theorem normalization : forall t T, empty |-- t : T -> SN t.
Proof.
  intros t T Hty.
  pose (sigma := fun x => tm_fvar x).
  assert (Hlc : forall x, locally_closed (sigma x)) by (intro x; constructor).
  assert (Henv : env_sem empty sigma). { intros x T' Hx. unfold empty in Hx. discriminate. }
  pose proof (fundamental empty t T Hty sigma Hlc Henv) as Htr.
  rewrite subst_id in Htr. apply tr_sn in Htr. exact Htr.
Qed.
```

Now let's think about the `tr` Fixpoint and whether Coq accepts it. The definition:

```
Fixpoint tr (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t =>
      locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall u, tr T1 u -> tr T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```

Coq guard checker: recursive calls `tr T1` and `tr T2` where T1, T2 are subterms of the match scrutinee. But the occurrence `tr T1 u` — Coq needs to see `tr T1` as a recursive call on subterm T1. Should be accepted. However, there's a subtlety: the return type is `tm -> Prop`; the recursive calls are at type `tm -> Prop`. The guard condition requires the recursive argument to be a subterm; T1 and T2 are variables bound by the pattern. Should be fine.

Actually, one issue: Coq's guard checker for nested recursive calls inside a lambda/forall in the body: the recursive call `tr T1 u` appears inside `forall u, tr T1 u -> tr T2 (tm_app t u)`. The occ occurrences are in a forall's body, which is fine (positive position? Guard checking doesn't require positivity for Fixpoints, only structural recursion; Prop-valued recursion with negative occurrences is allowed? Hmm, the guard checker checks recursive calls are on structurally smaller args; negativity isn't an issue for Fixpoint acceptance? Actually Coq rejects recursive definitions where the recursive call's result is used in a negative position? No—that's only for inductive definitions (strict positivity). Fixpoints can return Prop and use recursion negatively; e.g., `Fixpoint f (n:nat) : Prop := match n with 0 => True | S k => f k -> False end.` Is this accepted? Coq's guard condition is about recursive calls; the "negative occurrence" is fine for a Fixpoint? I believe Coq accepts it (the fixpoint is not used to build an inductive). Hmm, actually Coq might complain "Non strictly positive occurrence"? No, that's for Inductive. Fixpoints are fine.

      (iii) beta: if s = abs body, value w, then open body w: need SN. Hmm.
    This suggests proving SN(app s w) by induction on SN s and SN w, and then tr B via the closure lemma recursively... but we're in the middle of proving the closure lemma at type B with a different term. The induction hypothesis at B is exactly the closure lemma at type B, which requires "forall reducts of app s w, tr B". So we need tr B of each reduct of app s w:
      (i) app s' w: from tr T s' closure ✓.
      (ii) app s w': need tr B (app s w'). This has the same form as the goal, so apply the closure lemma at B *again* with the same s but different w'... circular unless we do induction on something decreasing (SN w?). Alternatively, note s is a value in this case (ST_App2 requires value s); if s = abs body, then app s w' with w related might be handled by the abstraction structure; if s = true/false, app s w' is stuck if w'...
      (iii) beta: s = abs body, w value: open body w. Need tr B (open body w). Hmm, where would that come from? The hypothesis "forall s', s --> s' -> tr T s'" doesn't cover beta (it's a step of app s w, not of s). 

  This closure lemma is too strong: it would assert tr (A->B) (abs body) from nothing about the body, which is false (e.g., the looping body). Indeed, if s = abs body is a value, it has no reducts, so the premise is vacuous, and the lemma would claim tr (A->B) (abs body) for all body — false. So the closure lemma is false. Good catch. 

So we cannot avoid analyzing the body/argument interplay. Back to tr_abs_app: we have Hbody : forall related v, tr T2 (open body v), which is the missing information. For non-value u, the term app abs u has only App2 reducts (no beta), so the closure lemma might work if restricted to terms that are not values and whose "beta potential" is captured... Hmm. Let's define a specialized closure for non-value s:

If s is lc, SN, not a value, and all reducts s' are tr T, is tr T s? For T=Bool: yes (SN given). For arrow: closure forall w related, tr B (app s w). Now app s w's reducts: App1 (s --> s', gives tr B via tr_app of tr T s'), App2 requires value s (no), beta requires s = abs (no). So the only reducts are App1 ones, which are in tr B. So by closure lemma at B (which is an IH, if we prove the lemma by induction on T), we need SN(app s w) and lc and all reducts tr B — and we have all reducts tr B, but we need SN. SN can be shown by induction on SN s and SN w: all reducts are in tr B hence SN; the App1 reducts are tr B ✓; App2/beta impossible since s not a value. Wait, if s is not a value, ST_App2 requires value s1 = s, so impossible; beta requires s = abs (value), impossible. App1: s --> s', then tr B (app s' w) via tr T s'. All reducts of app s w are SN. Then SN_intro gives SN. Then apply closure lemma at B (IH) to get tr B (app s w). 

So the lemma:
`tr_closure_nonvalue : forall T s, lc s -> SN s -> ~ value s -> (forall s', s --> s' -> tr T s') -> tr T s`
Proof by induction on T:
- Bool: lc, SN.
- Arrow A B: lc, SN, forall w related, tr B (app s w). Apply IH at B? No wait, the lemma at B requires: lc(app s w), SN(app s w), ~ value(app s w), and forall s'', app s w --> s'' -> tr B s''.
  - lc: yes.
  - ~value: app is not a value.
  - SN(app s w): by SN_intro: for each reduct r:
    - App1: s --> s', r = app s' w; tr B (app s' w) from tr T s' (arrow closure) gives SN.
    - App2: requires value s, contradiction.
    - beta: requires s = abs, which is a value, contradiction.
    So all reducts SN. ✓
  - all reducts r of app s w are tr B r:
    - App1: as above ✓.
    - App2/beta impossible ✓.
  Then IH at B (structurally smaller) gives tr B (app s w). ✓.
Great, this lemma works by induction on T.

#### tr_back_step details

`Lemma tr_back_step : forall T s s', tr T s' -> s --> s' -> locally_closed s -> tr T s.`
Induction on T.
- Bool: Htr : tr Bool s' = lc s' ∧ SN s'. Goal lc s ∧ SN s. lc s given; SN s from SN_back s s' Hstep Hsn.
- Arrow A B: Htr : lc s' ∧ SN s' ∧ closure. Goal lc s ∧ SN s ∧ (forall u, tr A u -> tr B (app s u)).
  - lc s given.
  - SN s from SN_back.
  - closure: for u, Hu: tr A u. We have tr B (app s' u) from Htr's closure Hu. Need tr B (app s u). We have app s u --> app s' u via ST_App1 Hstep (lc u from tr_lc Hu). Apply IHT2 (at B) with s := app s u, s' := app s' u, Htr := tr B (app s' u), Hstep, and lc (app s u) = lc_app Hlc_s (tr_lc Hu). ✓.
  
  Wait: IHT2 is the lemma at type B with the same statement: tr B s' -> s --> s' -> lc s -> tr B s. Yes.
